It won’t power down. I know that sounds kind of work-focused, but even when things are technically fine, it’s like my mind keeps pulling up another tab.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control during the day too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during the day too. It didn’t feel like one single day where everything flipped, more like it started then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say depressed, exactly. Um… I’m not totally sure. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.

Like, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. So… I don’t know that I’d say less enjoyment exactly. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is my sleep. It’s gotten really unreliable. I’ll be exhausted and go to bed thinking I should just pass out, but then I’m lying there replaying sales forecasts and team issues, like conversations from the day or what might blow up tomorrow.\n\nAnd then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and my brain just… starts again. It won’t power down. I know that sounds kind of work-focused, but even when things are technically fine, it’s like my mind keeps pulling up another tab.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control during the day too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during the day too. I’ll literally tell myself, “Brenda, you already made the plan, let it go,” and for a second I think I’ve handled it.\n\nBut then five minutes later I’m back in it, like another loop starts. I’ll check one worry off and then suddenly there are three more—like, okay, what about that client, what about the numbers, what if someone on the team is upset… It’s like whack-a-mole in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually energetic, like in a good way? I don’t know. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where I slept less, but usually it’s because I’m stressed or my brain won’t stop, not because I feel great or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI guess sometimes if there’s a big work thing coming up, I can run on adrenaline for a day or two, but then I feel it. Like, I’m tired. I’m not suddenly superhuman or anything. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Besides work, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it’s not just work. Work is the loudest one, probably because it’s in my face all day, but my brain doesn’t really stop there.\n\nLike, I’ll go from worrying about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is secretly about to quit, and then somehow I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal, or—this is stupid—but if someone takes too long to answer an email I start building this whole story around it.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like I need to call the plumber because our sink made this weird noise last week, and suddenly that feels connected to everything else, like one little delay is going to turn into a huge problem. But yeah, if I had to mark when it began, it was around that change. It didn’t feel like one single day where everything flipped, more like it started then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say depressed, exactly. Like, not in the way I picture that word, where you can’t get out of bed or everything feels pointless.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel kind of low or worn down, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just because things have been nonstop, and then I get annoyed with myself for even labeling it. Sorry—my sister always says I try to “logic” my way out of feelings, which is probably true.\n\nBut over the past couple weeks… mostly I’d say I’ve felt stressed and tired of my own brain more than sad. Not really down all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s been going on since that sales division reorganization, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because everything was in flux—you know, new reporting lines, people acting like they understood the new structure when nobody really did, all those weird meetings where everyone says “alignment” fifteen times—but the worrying didn’t really fade once that settled down. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment out of things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. But I don’t think the show itself feels bad or pointless or anything. Same with dinner with friends—I can still have a good time once I’m there, though getting there sometimes feels like a whole production.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d say less enjoyment exactly. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. But I don’t think the show itself feels bad or pointless or anything. Same with dinner with friends—I can still have a good time once I’m there, though getting there sometimes feels like a whole production.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d say less enjoyment exactly. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I feel sort of wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body thinks I’m sitting in a pipeline review even when I’m just at home eating dinner or trying to watch TV. Which is ridiculous, because nothing is actually happening, but my shoulders will be up around my ears and I don’t even notice until they start aching.\n\nI keep trying to relax them, like consciously dropping them, stretching, all that, and then five minutes later they’re tight again. So yes, definitely on edge physically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It won’t power down. I know that sounds kind of work-focused, but even when things are technically fine, it’s like my mind keeps pulling up another tab.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control during the day too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during the day too. I’ll literally tell myself, “Brenda, you already made the plan, let it go,” and for a second I think I’ve handled it.\n\nBut then five minutes later I’m back in it, like another loop starts. I’ll check one worry off and then suddenly there are three more—like, okay, what about that client, what about the numbers, what if someone on the team is upset… It’s like whack-a-mole in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually energetic, like in a good way? I don’t know. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where I slept less, but usually it’s because I’m stressed or my brain won’t stop, not because I feel great or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI guess sometimes if there’s a big work thing coming up, I can run on adrenaline for a day or two, but then I feel it. Kind of, but it’s not exactly worrying about sleep at first. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 too, and it’s the same thing, like the whole sales pipeline has apparently decided to meet in my head at that hour.

Work is the loudest one, probably because it’s in my face all day, but my brain doesn’t really stop there.\n\nLike, I’ll go from worrying about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is secretly about to quit, and then somehow I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal, or—this is stupid—but if someone takes too long to answer an email I start building this whole story around it.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like I need to call the plumber because our sink made this weird noise last week, and suddenly that feels connected to everything else, like one little delay is going to turn into a huge problem. Yeah, that happens a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on, say, a proposal or even just reading an email, and then my brain is back on whatever I was worrying about.

something major.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I can tell myself, 'Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go,' but five minutes later I'm back in another loop, checking one concern off only to have three more pop up.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: I feel wound up almost all the time, like I'm sitting in a pipeline review even when I'm just at home eating dinner, and my I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 too, and it’s the same thing, like the whole sales pipeline has apparently decided to meet in my head at that hour.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled back into your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I’m not sure I’d say sad or hopeless most of the day. I mean, I get… worn down, I guess, and sometimes I’ll have a rough afternoon where everything feels like too much.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is my sleep. It’s gotten really unreliable. I’ll be exhausted and go to bed thinking I should just pass out, but then I’m lying there replaying sales forecasts and team issues, like conversations from the day or what might blow up tomorrow.\n\nAnd then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and my brain just… starts again. It won’t power down. I know that sounds kind of work-focused, but even when things are technically fine, it’s like my mind keeps pulling up another tab.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control during the day too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during the day too. I’ll literally tell myself, “Brenda, you already made the plan, let it go,” and for a second I think I’ve handled it.\n\nBut then five minutes later I’m back in it, like another loop starts. I’ll check one worry off and then suddenly there are three more—like, okay, what about that client, what about the numbers, what if someone on the team is upset… It’s like whack-a-mole in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually energetic, like in a good way? I don’t know. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where I slept less, but usually it’s because I’m stressed or my brain won’t stop, not because I feel great or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI guess sometimes if there’s a big work thing coming up, I can run on adrenaline for a day or two, but then I feel it. Like, I’m tired. I’m not suddenly superhuman or anything. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Besides work, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it’s not just work. Work is the loudest one, probably because it’s in my face all day, but my brain doesn’t really stop there.\n\nLike, I’ll go from worrying about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is secretly about to quit, and then somehow I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal, or—this is stupid—but if someone takes too long to answer an email I start building this whole story around it.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like I need to call the plumber because our sink made this weird noise last week, and suddenly that feels connected to everything else, like one little delay is going to turn into a huge problem. So… yes. It jumps around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it really started building after the sales division reorganization… that was about thirteen months ago now, which is weird because it feels both recent and like forever ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stuff, because everyone was kind of scrambling and the reporting lines changed, and I remember spending way too much time trying to figure out who owned what—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, if I had to mark when it began, it was around that change. It didn’t feel like one single day where everything flipped, more like it started then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say depressed, exactly. Like, not in the way I picture that word, where you can’t get out of bed or everything feels pointless.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel kind of low or worn down, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just because things have been nonstop, and then I get annoyed with myself for even labeling it. Sorry—my sister always says I try to “logic” my way out of feelings, which is probably true.\n\nBut over the past couple weeks… mostly I’d say I’ve felt stressed and tired of my own brain more than sad. Not really down all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s been going on since that sales division reorganization, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because everything was in flux—you know, new reporting lines, people acting like they understood the new structure when nobody really did, all those weird meetings where everyone says “alignment” fifteen times—but the worrying didn’t really fade once that settled down. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment out of things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I feel sort of wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body thinks I’m sitting in a pipeline review even when I’m just at home eating dinner or trying to watch TV. Yeah… I think I have. I don’t love admitting that, but yes.

Like at work, I’ll snap at one of my reps over something tiny, like a CRM update being incomplete or formatted weird, and in the moment it feels urgent, like, “Why is this not done right?” But afterward I’m sitting there thinking, Brenda, that was not worth that tone.

And at home too—my husband will leave dishes in the sink, which, I mean, annoying, sure, but not exactly a federal case. And I’ll react like it is. Then ten minutes later I’m apologizing or just feeling awful because I know my reaction was way bigger than the actual thing.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is my sleep. It’s gotten really unreliable. I’ll be exhausted and go to bed thinking I should just pass out, but then I’m lying there replaying sales forecasts and team issues, like conversations from the day or what might blow up tomorrow.\n\nAnd then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and my brain just… starts again. It won’t power down. I know that sounds kind of work-focused, but even when things are technically fine, it’s like my mind keeps pulling up another tab.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worrying feels hard to control during the day too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during the day too. I’ll literally tell myself, “Brenda, you already made the plan, let it go,” and for a second I think I’ve handled it.\n\nBut then five minutes later I’m back in it, like another loop starts. I’ll check one worry off and then suddenly there are three more—like, okay, what about that client, what about the numbers, what if someone on the team is upset… It’s like whack-a-mole in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual without feeling tired?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually energetic, like in a good way? I don’t know. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where I slept less, but usually it’s because I’m stressed or my brain won’t stop, not because I feel great or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI guess sometimes if there’s a big work thing coming up, I can run on adrenaline for a day or two, but then I feel it. Like, I’m tired. I’m not suddenly superhuman or anything. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Besides work, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it’s not just work. Work is the loudest one, probably because it’s in my face all day, but my brain doesn’t really stop there.\n\nLike, I’ll go from worrying about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is secretly about to quit, and then somehow I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal, or—this is stupid—but if someone takes too long to answer an email I start building this whole story around it.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like I need to call the plumber because our sink made this weird noise last week, and suddenly that feels connected to everything else, like one little delay is going to turn into a huge problem. So… yes. It jumps around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it really started building after the sales division reorganization… that was about thirteen months ago now, which is weird because it feels both recent and like forever ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stuff, because everyone was kind of scrambling and the reporting lines changed, and I remember spending way too much time trying to figure out who owned what—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, if I had to mark when it began, it was around that change. It didn’t feel like one single day where everything flipped, more like it started then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say depressed, exactly. Like, not in the way I picture that word, where you can’t get out of bed or everything feels pointless.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel kind of low or worn down, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just because things have been nonstop, and then I get annoyed with myself for even labeling it. Sorry—my sister always says I try to “logic” my way out of feelings, which is probably true.\n\nBut over the past couple weeks… mostly I’d say I’ve felt stressed and tired of my own brain more than sad. Not really down all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s been going on since that sales division reorganization, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because everything was in flux—you know, new reporting lines, people acting like they understood the new structure when nobody really did, all those weird meetings where everyone says “alignment” fifteen times—but the worrying didn’t really fade once that settled down. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment out of things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. But I don’t think the show itself feels bad or pointless or anything. Same with dinner with friends—I can still have a good time once I’m there, though getting there sometimes feels like a whole production.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d say less enjoyment exactly. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I feel sort of wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body thinks I’m sitting in a pipeline review even when I’m just at home eating dinner or trying to watch TV. Which is ridiculous, because nothing is actually happening, but my shoulders will be up around my ears and I don’t even notice until they start aching.\n\nI keep trying to relax them, like consciously dropping them, stretching, all that, and then five minutes later they’re tight again. So yes, definitely on edge physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, some afternoons I hit that point where I’m staring at my laptop and rereading the same thing, but then I also think—well, who doesn’t do that? Especially in sales, because there’s always some deck or forecast or someone asking for “just a quick update,” which is never quick.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed fatigue as a big thing specifically. Not in the way where I’m like, “Why am I exhausted all day?” It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is the main problem that you get stuck worrying about not sleeping?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s not exactly worrying about sleep at first. So yes, definitely on edge physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, some afternoons I hit that point where I’m staring at my laptop and rereading the same thing, but then I also think—well, who doesn’t do that? Especially in sales, because there’s always some deck or forecast or someone asking for “just a quick update,” which is never quick.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed fatigue as a big thing specifically. Not in the way where I’m like, “Why am I exhausted all day?” It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is the main problem that you get stuck worrying about not sleeping?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s not exactly worrying about sleep at first. But the first thing is usually my brain just… not powering down. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 too, and it’s the same thing, like the whole sales pipeline has apparently decided to meet in my head at that hour.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled back into your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on, say, a proposal or even just reading an email, and then my brain is back on whatever I was worrying about.\n\nI can tell myself, “Brenda, you’ve done the plan, let it go,” and I’ll mean it. Like I’ll actually take a breath and think, okay, that’s handled. But then five minutes later I’m in another loop—one concern gets checked off and then three more pop up.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ll look productive, I guess, sitting there with my laptop open, but mentally I’m… not fully there. I’m off chasing the next “what if.”\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad or hopeless most of the day. I mean, I get… worn down, I guess, and sometimes I’ll have a rough afternoon where everything feels like too much.\n\nBut “hopeless” feels like a strong word. Sad too—I don’t know. Like, I can still get distracted by normal things, like my friend sent me this ridiculous voice note the other day and I laughed for way too long, and then I felt kind of weird because I’d been so tense before that.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t love admitting that, but yes.\n\nLike at work, I’ll snap at one of my reps over something tiny, like a CRM update being incomplete or formatted weird, and in the moment it feels urgent, like, “Why is this not done right?” But afterward I’m sitting there thinking, Brenda, that was not worth that tone.\n\nAnd at home too—my husband will leave dishes in the sink, which, I mean, annoying, sure, but not exactly a federal case. I mean, I’ve definitely had times where I slept less, but usually it’s because I’m stressed or my brain won’t stop, not because I feel great or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI guess sometimes if there’s a big work thing coming up, I can run on adrenaline for a day or two, but then I feel it. Like, I’m tired. I’m not suddenly superhuman or anything. Sorry, I’m probably over-explaining. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Besides work, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of your life too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it’s not just work. Work is the loudest one, probably because it’s in my face all day, but my brain doesn’t really stop there.\n\nLike, I’ll go from worrying about whether we’re going to hit the launch numbers to wondering if someone on my team is secretly about to quit, and then somehow I’m thinking about the mortgage, or a client renewal, or—this is stupid—but if someone takes too long to answer an email I start building this whole story around it.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like I need to call the plumber because our sink made this weird noise last week, and suddenly that feels connected to everything else, like one little delay is going to turn into a huge problem. So… yes. It jumps around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I think it really started building after the sales division reorganization… that was about thirteen months ago now, which is weird because it feels both recent and like forever ago.\n\nAt first I thought it was just normal adjustment stuff, because everyone was kind of scrambling and the reporting lines changed, and I remember spending way too much time trying to figure out who owned what—sorry, that’s not really the point. But yeah, if I had to mark when it began, it was around that change. It didn’t feel like one single day where everything flipped, more like it started then and just kept growing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say depressed, exactly. Like, not in the way I picture that word, where you can’t get out of bed or everything feels pointless.\n\nI’ve had moments where I feel kind of low or worn down, but then I start thinking maybe that’s just because things have been nonstop, and then I get annoyed with myself for even labeling it. Sorry—my sister always says I try to “logic” my way out of feelings, which is probably true.\n\nBut over the past couple weeks… mostly I’d say I’ve felt stressed and tired of my own brain more than sad. Not really down all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s been going on since that sales division reorganization, which was about thirteen months ago now.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just because everything was in flux—you know, new reporting lines, people acting like they understood the new structure when nobody really did, all those weird meetings where everyone says “alignment” fifteen times—but the worrying didn’t really fade once that settled down. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment out of things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I still do things I usually like, but sometimes I’m distracted while I’m doing them, so it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them less or just not really paying attention.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show I normally like and then realize I’ve been on my phone looking at… actually, it’s embarrassing, like random work-adjacent stuff, and then I miss half the episode. But I don’t think the show itself feels bad or pointless or anything. Same with dinner with friends—I can still have a good time once I’m there, though getting there sometimes feels like a whole production.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d say less enjoyment exactly. Maybe more like I’m not always fully present for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I feel sort of wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body thinks I’m sitting in a pipeline review even when I’m just at home eating dinner or trying to watch TV. Which is ridiculous, because nothing is actually happening, but my shoulders will be up around my ears and I don’t even notice until they start aching.\n\nI keep trying to relax them, like consciously dropping them, stretching, all that, and then five minutes later they’re tight again. So yes, definitely on edge physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get tired, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, some afternoons I hit that point where I’m staring at my laptop and rereading the same thing, but then I also think—well, who doesn’t do that? Especially in sales, because there’s always some deck or forecast or someone asking for “just a quick update,” which is never quick.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed fatigue as a big thing specifically. Not in the way where I’m like, “Why am I exhausted all day?” It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you can’t sleep, is the main problem that you get stuck worrying about not sleeping?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but it’s not exactly worrying about sleep at first. It starts with work stuff—like I’ll be exhausted and go to bed thinking, great, I’ll finally crash, and then my brain starts replaying forecasts, or whether someone on my team is going to miss a number, or some issue I should’ve handled differently.\n\nAnd then, yeah, after a while I do start thinking, okay, now I’m not sleeping, tomorrow’s going to be a mess. But the first thing is usually my brain just… not powering down. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 too, and it’s the same thing, like the whole sales pipeline has apparently decided to meet in my head at that hour.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind keeps getting pulled back into your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. I’ll be trying to focus on, say, a proposal or even just reading an email, and then my brain is back on whatever I was worrying about.\n\nI can tell myself, “Brenda, you’ve done the plan, let it go,” and I’ll mean it. Like I’ll actually take a breath and think, okay, that’s handled. But then five minutes later I’m in another loop—one concern gets checked off and then three more pop up.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ll look productive, I guess, sitting there with my laptop open, but mentally I’m… not fully there. I’m off chasing the next “what if.”\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt sad or hopeless most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad or hopeless most of the day. I mean, I get… worn down, I guess, and sometimes I’ll have a rough afternoon where everything feels like too much.\n\nBut “hopeless” feels like a strong word. Sad too—I don’t know. Like, I can still get distracted by normal things, like my friend sent me this ridiculous voice note the other day and I laughed for way too long, and then I felt kind of weird because I’d been so tense before that.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I don’t love admitting that, but yes.\n\nLike at work, I’ll snap at one of my reps over something tiny, like a CRM update being incomplete or formatted weird, and in the moment it feels urgent, like, “Why is this not done right?” But afterward I’m sitting there thinking, Brenda, that was not worth that tone.\n\nAnd at home too—my husband will leave dishes in the sink, which, I mean, annoying, sure, but not exactly a federal case. And I’ll react like it is. Then ten minutes later I’m apologizing or just feeling awful because I know my reaction was way bigger than the actual thing.\n\nDoctor: On nights when your mind is relatively calm, do you still have trouble falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, sometimes if I’m actually calm, sleep comes a little easier, but that’s not what’s been happening most nights.\n\nI’ll go to bed completely exhausted, like I can barely keep my eyes open, and then the second I’m in bed my brain starts replaying sales forecasts, or something with the team, or whether I missed some issue that’s going to become a bigger problem. And then even if I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and it’s like my mind just turns back on.\n\nIt’s annoying because my husband can just… fall asleep in two minutes, which feels illegal somehow. And I’m lying there doing math in my head about projections or thinking about a conversation I need to have with someone. So yeah, my sleep has gotten really unreliable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
